TL;DR: The study investigates the impact of decoherence processes on quantum annealing by treating spontaneous decay and dephasing as independent factors, demonstrating how to optimize performance across varying noise levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding the effects of decoherence on quantum-annealing processes to improve optimization outcomes in Rydberg-atom systems.

Method: The authors analyzed the optimization of the maximum independent set on random graphs by mapping the effect of spontaneous decay ($Γ$) and dephasing ($γ$) across the $(Γ,γ)$ plane, and re-optimizing the annealing time accordingly.

Result: The mean approximation ratio is shown to follow the relationship $u=κΓ+γ$, where $κ$ is approximately 8.05, revealing that standard isotropic models misrepresent the decoherence effect by a significant margin.

Conclusion: The study concludes that a calibrated joint tuning of driven parameters can recover substantial performance losses without additional hardware costs, emphasizing the importance of the optimized decay schedule dependent on the device's characteristics.

Abstract: Numerical studies of noisy Rydberg-atom optimization almost universally compress decoherence into a single scalar, silently pricing spontaneous decay ($Γ$) and dephasing ($γ$) alike. We treat the two as independent axes, mapping a quantum-annealing heuristic for unit-disk maximum independent set on 20 random $N=10$ graphs across the $(Γ,γ)$ plane, with the annealing time re-optimized at every point. The mean approximation ratio does collapse onto one scalar, but onto $u=κΓ+γ$ with $κ=8.05\pm0.5$ (stat) $\pm1.1$ (syst); the isotropic $Γ+γ$ fails by a factor of 30 in residual. First-order perturbation theory reproduces $κ$ from noiseless propagation alone and gives the mechanism: the objective is diagonal in the basis of the dephasing operator, so dephasing cannot change the answer once the drive is off, and a single driven atom already has $κ\simeq8.5$. The exchange rate is thus a property of the protocol as much as of the platform: the ramp-down fraction moves it between 2.3 and 16.3. At a fixed schedule it is stable across system sizes, interaction strengths, and estimators. Because the whole cost model is noiseless, a schedule can be tuned to a device's channel mixture without any noisy simulation: jointly tuning the drive ramp-down and the sweep's detuning ramp recovers about a third of the Markovian damage at no hardware cost, and the ramp the noise-aware objective selects is not the one noiseless optimization would choose. Per unit rate decay is eightfold the dearer channel, but the measured $T_1$ enters weighted by its branching ratio to the ground state ($b\approx0.4$ for the calibrated device), which leaves the two Lindblad channels comparably costly at a present-day operating point. One-parameter noise models remain serviceable, provided the parameter is $u$.

</details>


### [96] [Exact Resource Laws for Passive Wavelength Routing in Entanglement Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29911)
*Ekta Panwar,Gilberto Borges,Saeid Salari,Kartik Kakade,Samgeeth Puliyil,Peter Rapčan,Mario Ziman,Djeylan Aktas*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper addresses the optimization of wavelength assignments in entanglement-based quantum communication networks, focusing on resource constraints and proposing solutions for connectivity and spectral efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enhance scalability in multiuser quantum communication through entanglement-based networks while addressing the limitations of existing wavelength-allocation schemes.

Method: The authors use a connectivity graph to frame the wavelength assignment problem as a resource-optimization challenge, providing exact solutions for various network configurations and analyzing the impact of fan-out and receiver load on spectral efficiency.

Result: The study presents a balanced binary hierarchy that minimizes spectral-layer count and maximizes efficiency for complete networks, while also calculating the necessary pair-generation rates and implementing a BBM92 QKD framework.

Conclusion: The proposed framework facilitates the design and optimization of passive entanglement architectures for quantum key distribution, effectively navigating the trade-offs between spectral efficiency, receiver load, and network constraints.

Abstract: Entanglement-based networks provide a scalable framework for multiuser quantum communication by passively routing spectrally correlated photon pairs across interconnected nodes. Several wavelength-allocation schemes have been demonstrated experimentally, but these designs do not yet give a general way to determine how spectral use, receiver load, repeated connections, and fan-out constrain one another. We address this problem through the network's connectivity graph, where the wavelength assignment becomes a resource-optimization problem. For one-sided fan-out, assigning each link to a center and grouping links with the same center gives an exact optimization for arbitrary networks and fan-out limits. We solve this for complete networks and for complete networks in which every user has one excluded partner. Allowing both conjugate wavelengths to fan out changes the resource landscape: a balanced binary hierarchy attains the minimum spectral-layer count for a complete network while reducing the maximum receiver load to logarithmic in the number of users. An eight-user complete network then makes explicit the competing roles of spectral efficiency, receiver load, redundancy, and fan-out. We include the passive-splitter loss and the dependence of the key rate on the delivered pair flux to determine the minimum total pair-generation rate required to meet the prescribed targets. Finally, we formulate the corresponding BBM92 quantum key distribution (QKD) secret-key-rate analysis for a continuous-wave-pumped broadband source, with true and accidental coincidences evaluated between detector channels at the two endpoint users and relative layer pair-generation rates fixed by the source spectrum. This framework, therefore, provides a direct route from exact network resource laws to the design and comparison of passive entanglement architectures under experimentally specified hardware constraints.

</details>


### [97] [Emergence of Strategic Equilibria from Transverse Field Ising Hamiltonian Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29926)
*Teena Thomas,S. Balakrishnan*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper explores quantum game theory using an operator-based approach tied to the transverse-field quantum Ising model, demonstrating a quantum advantage through tunable entanglement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enhance quantum game theory by leveraging operator-based formulations and establish a stronger connection to physical systems.

Method: Utilizing Hamiltonian-driven dynamics from the transverse-field quantum Ising model to create entangling operators in quantum games.

Result: Demonstrates that the approach yields clear quantum advantages compared to classical strategies, with entanglement being tunable via Hamiltonian parameters.

Conclusion: The research provides a framework for quantum game design that is directly applicable in hardware settings, improving the practical implementation of quantum games.

Abstract: Game theory studies strategic decision-making among rational agents, and many classical games can be mapped onto interaction models such as the Ising model. Quantum game theory extends this framework by allowing players to exploit quantum superposition and entanglement. In this work, we study quantum games using an operator-based formulation derived from the transverse-field quantum Ising model. We show that the Hamiltonian-driven dynamics naturally generate entangling operator which resolve the dilemma in the game settings. This leads to a clear quantum advantage over classical outcomes. Unlike standard quantization schemes based on fixed entangling gates, the present approach enables tunable entanglement, controlled directly by physical Hamiltonian parameters, providing a hardware-relevant perspective on quantum game design.

</details>


### [98] [Exact Entanglement Swapping through Single-Occupancy Measurements in Gaussian Fermion States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29933)
*Jiyuan Fang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper analyzes the entanglement structure of a conditional state in fermionic systems, highlighting dependencies on initial states and mechanisms for computing entanglement entropy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the entanglement properties of conditional states arising from measurements in fermionic systems and their implications for entanglement swapping.

Method: The authors measure $m$ rungs in two copies of a half-filled free fermion Gaussian state, post-selecting a normalized single-fermion state. They analyze the resulting entanglement structure and calculate the entanglement entropy and success probability.

Result: The conditional wavefunction retains Gaussian characteristics and leads to a factorization into orthogonal modes. The entanglement entropy is found to be $S=m h_2(|v|^2)$, and the success probability depends solely on initial correlations, independent of the measurement outcomes ($u$ and $v$).

Conclusion: The findings reveal that the entanglement entropy is universal for a given number of measurements, and specific post-selection can maximize entanglement swapping.

Abstract: We determine the exact entanglement structure of the conditional state obtained by measuring $m$ corresponding rungs ($m\leq N/2$) in two identical copies of an arbitrary half-filled free fermion Gaussian state and post-selecting the same normalized single-fermion state ($|ψ\rangle = u|10\rangle+v|01\rangle$, where 0 and 1 denote the fermion occupancy on each sites) on each rung. For $m<N/2$, the conditional wavefunction generally depends on the initial state. Nevertheless, whenever the selected outcome has nonzero probability, the state on the unmeasured sites remains Gaussian and factorizes exactly into $N-m$ different orthogonal modes including $m$ inter-copy entangling modes and $N-2m$ spectator modes localized in one copy. Consequently, the entanglement entropy between the unmeasured parts of the two copies is $S=m h_2(|v|^2)$, independent of the initial state, where $h_2(x)=-x\ln x-(1-x)\ln(1-x)$. The success probability is given by $P_m=\det C_L\det(I_m-C_L)=\det(C_{LR}C_{RL})$, determined solely by the initial correlations and independent of ($u$,$v$). Equal-weight Bell post-selection serves as a special case that achieves the maximal entanglement swapping.

</details>


### [99] [The MS Unidirectional Current as a Generalization of the ABC Absorption Current](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29969)
*Avi Marchewka*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the relationship between Robin boundary conditions for scalar particles and the generalized Marchewka--Schuss unidirectional current, showing that all Robin conditions fit within a broader framework of MS conditions, while also identifying limitations of the Robin approach in terms of absorption efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore the relationship between Robin boundary conditions and the generalized MS current in the context of detecting scalar particles in one-dimensional spaces.

Method: Comparison of Robin boundary conditions characterized by the parameter $κ$ with the generalized Marchewka--Schuss current characterized by a spectral function $λ(k)$, demonstrating their relationship and differences in absorption profiles.

Result: Every Robin condition is shown to correspond to a specific MS calibration, enabling complete absorption modeling; however, the MS framework allows for greater adaptability, particularly in achieving unit absorption efficiency across all wave numbers.

Conclusion: The generalized MS detector process not only encompasses the Robin absorption family but also provides a more versatile framework for boundary detection, addressing its limitations.

Abstract: For a free scalar particle in one spatial dimension, in a single-pass half-line geometry, we compare the absorbing Robin boundary condition with the generalized Marchewka--Schuss (MS) unidirectional-current family. Robin absorption is characterized by a single parameter $κ>0$: once $κ$ is specified, its momentum-dependent absorption profile is fixed. In the generalized MS construction, by contrast, the detector response is described by a spectral function $λ(k)$, subject to the positivity and subnormalization conditions of the detection law. We show that every Robin choice of $κ$ corresponds to the particular MS calibration $λ_κ(k)=πκ/(k+κ)^2$, which reproduces the complete Robin absorption current for every admissible one-sided spectral amplitude and at every time. Thus the entire one-parameter Robin absorption family is contained within the generalized MS family. The MS construction is more general: the choice $λ_{\rm full}(k)=π/(4k)$ gives unit absorption efficiency for every wave number, which cannot be achieved by any fixed Robin parameter. In this precise sense, the generalized MS detector process extends the Robin absorbing-boundary family. We also compare their asymptotic behavior in the ballistic fixed-ray regime and in the fixed-distance late-time limit.

</details>


### [100] [Frame phase retrievability and state distinguishability of quantum channels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30006)
*Deguang Han,Kai Liu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This survey discusses frame phase retrieval's significance in identifying pure states and preserving information through quantum channels, linking injectivity of channels to phase retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how frame phase retrieval aids in pure-state identification and the preservation of information through quantum channels.

Method: Utilizes Choi-rank-two linear combinations of Kraus operators, analyzes higher-rank relative spectra and structural obstructions, and distinguishes various forms of quantum information tasks while exploring twirling channels.

Result: Establishes a connection between phase retrieval and channel injectivity on pure states, and highlights differences among identification methods in quantum information.

Conclusion: Twirling channels offer a structured approach to understanding various quantum phenomena related to phase retrieval, showcasing how group representations can elucidate this relationship.

Abstract: This survey introduces the role of frame phase retrieval in pure-state identification and information preservation by quantum channels. For unit vectors, the lift $x\mapsto xx^*$ identifies vectors that differ only by a global phase with the same rank-one quantum state, converts frame intensities into linear functionals of the lifted state, and makes the adjoint pullback of output observables an operator-valued measurement on the input system. From this viewpoint, a channel is phase retrievable exactly when it is injective on pure states. We develop this correspondence through Choi-rank-two linear combinations of Kraus operators, higher-rank relative spectra, structural obstructions, and constructions with prescribed Choi rank. We distinguish injective identification from full tomography, perfect one-shot discrimination, zero-error classical communication, and exact quantum correction. Twirling channels then provide a structured setting in which commutants, irreducible dimensions, multiplicities, orbit-frame orthogonality, coding indices, and phase-retrievable subspaces can be read from group representations.

</details>


### [101] [Newtonian Gravitational Curvature-Induced Entanglement Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30075)
*Mughees Ahmad Khan,Asad Ali,M. I Hussain,H. Kuniyil,Saif Al-Kuwari*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper explores how gravitational curvature influences nonlocal quantum correlations in a qubit-mechanical device, revealing methods for curvature estimation and entanglement generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the role of gravitational curvature in quantum systems, particularly its effect on entangling interactions in hybrid qubit-mechanical devices.

Method: The authors analyze a qubit-mechanical system's dynamics under gravitational influence, deriving limits for curvature estimation and utilizing a parity-based measurement strategy.

Result: They demonstrate that gravitational curvature can be used to control entanglement, with findings indicating that mechanical damping and qubit dephasing have distinct impacts on entanglement visibility.

Conclusion: The research provides a novel approach to curvature sensing and highlights the potential of using mechanical oscillators in understanding gravitational effects in quantum mechanics.

Abstract: We show that gravitational curvature can control the generation of nonlocal quantum correlations in a hybrid qubit-mechanical device. The tidal field of a nearby source mass modifies the susceptibility of a shared mechanical oscillator, thereby tuning an oscillator-mediated qubit-qubit interaction and the resulting entangling phase. An exact treatment of the dynamics reveals stroboscopic geometric gates whose accumulated phase is directly sensitive to gravitational curvature. Treating the curvature as an unknown parameter, we derive the ultimate quantum limit for its estimation and identify a parity-based measurement that saturates this bound. Solving the full master equation with the mechanical mode retained explicitly, we find that thermal occupation of the mediator suppresses entanglement between the closure times but is undone at each closure, exactly in the unitary limit for any initial mechanical temperature, so that ground-state cooling of the oscillator is not a prerequisite for the protocol. Mechanical damping and qubit dephasing behave differently: they leak branch information irreversibly to the environment, and it is the heating and dephasing rates, rather than the bath occupation alone, that limit the entanglement visibility and the number of usable interrogation loops. In contrast to gravity-mediated entanglement proposals, entanglement is generated by the mechanical oscillator while gravity acts solely as a classical control field. We quantify the achievable curvature parameters; all curvature dependencies are analytic, allowing for exact rescaling of the results. The scheme therefore demonstrates gravitational control of a quantum interaction and provides a route to curvature sensing based on a nonlocal two-qubit phase rather than on local phase measurements.

</details>


### [102] [Data and code for collision-model Dicke-state preparation: depth-fidelity frontiers, circuit costs, and superconducting-processor measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30176)
*Duc-Kha Vu,Minh Tam Nguyen,Şahin K. Özdemir,Özgür E. Müstecaplıoğlu,Fatih Ozaydin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a dataset and code for preparing Dicke states using a collision-based quantum protocol, addressing challenges in achieving high fidelity with varying qubit numbers and excitation levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dicke states are vital for applications in quantum sensing and networking, but high-fidelity preparation is still difficult as system size increases.

Method: The authors developed a collision-based quantum protocol to prepare Dicke states, and created a dataset covering 5 to 14 qubits with different excitation numbers.

Result: The dataset includes fidelity measurements as circuit depth increases and resource estimates, along with experimental data from a 54-qubit superconducting processor.

Conclusion: This resource provides a benchmark for evaluating fidelity, circuit cost, and noise, aiding future studies on Dicke states.

Abstract: Dicke states are multipartite entangled states in which a fixed number of quantum excitations is coherently shared among many qubits. Originally introduced in the context of cooperative emission and superradiance, they are now important resources for quantum sensing, networking, and collective quantum phenomena. Preparing prescribed Dicke states with high fidelity, however, remains challenging, particularly as the system size and excitation number increase. Here we present an open dataset and accompanying code for preparing Dicke states using a collision-based quantum protocol. The dataset covers systems from five to fourteen qubits over a broad range of excitation numbers and records how the best-found noiseless preparation fidelity changes with circuit depth. It also provides circuit-resource estimates and experimental measurements for selected states on the 54-qubit IQM Emerald superconducting processor. The accompanying code reproduces the processed data and validation checks, providing a reusable benchmark for studying the trade-off between state-preparation fidelity, circuit cost, and hardware noise.

</details>


### [103] [SpiderLS: Leveraging Full ZX Reduction for Lattice Surgery Compilation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30228)
*Hyungseok Kim,Changheon Lee,Seungjik Kim,Enhyeok Jang,Youngmin Kim,Seungwoo Choi,Hanbit Lee,Sungho Pyun,Won Woo Ro*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: SpiderLS improves lattice surgery compilation by fully utilizing ZX-reduction, leading to significant reductions in spacetime volume and compilation time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The need for efficient translation of fault-tolerant quantum programs into surface codes necessitates a focus on optimizing spatial and temporal resources during execution.

Method: SpiderLS enhances previous ZX-diagram compilation methods by employing full ZX reduction and applying multiple compiler passes for execution order, multi-target operation generation, and conversion to Pauli-product measurements.

Result: SpiderLS achieves an average reduction of 49.2% in spacetime volume and 99.8% in compilation time over previous ZX-based compilers across various workloads.

Conclusion: The approach significantly optimizes lattice surgery compilation, suggesting better feasibility for fault-tolerant quantum computing.

Abstract: Lattice surgery compilation plays a central role in translating fault-tolerant quantum programs into efficient surface code realizations, where both spatial and temporal resources directly determine the cost of execution. Recent work has demonstrated the benefits of using ZX-diagrams as an intermediate representation for lattice surgery compilation, enabling semantics-preserving transformations that reduce spacetime cost. However, existing compilation restricts ZX reduction to preserve diagram structures that can be directly embedded as lattice surgery junctions. We present SpiderLS, which extends prior approach by leveraging full ZX reduction. To translate the resulting diagram into executable lattice surgery operations, SpiderLS applies a sequence of compiler passes that derives an execution order, generates target code by grouping compatible interactions into multi-target operations, and lowers the target code to Pauli-product measurements. The resulting explicit patch and Pauli-boundary requirements guide logical scheduling and structure-aware spacetime routing. Across representative algorithmic and random workloads, SpiderLS achieves average reductions of 49.2% in spacetime volume and 99.8% in compilation time compared with the prior ZX-based compiler.

</details>


### [104] [Modular-Annihilator Parent Hamiltonians for Purified Gibbs States: Spectral Design and Controlled Approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30272)
*Changhao Yi,Jun Takahashi,Cunlu Zhou*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This study explores the construction of Hamiltonians and Lindbladians linked to purified Gibbs states using modular annihilators, highlighting advantages in free-fermion cases and a Krylov-Lanczos method for interacting systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research aims to connect finite-temperature physics with ground-state methods through purified Gibbs states and enhance the understanding of Hamiltonian structures.

Method: The study employs finite sum-of-squares constructions of parent Hamiltonians and Lindbladians from modular annihilators, analyzing free-fermion Hamiltonians and introducing a Krylov-Lanczos scheme for interacting systems.

Result: For free-fermion Hamiltonians, a specific choice of the symmetric matrix S leads to rapid mixing and optimal spectral properties, while a Krylov-Lanczos approach addresses ground-state approximations in interacting scenarios.

Conclusion: The findings reveal that the constructed Hamiltonians exhibit improved spectral properties, and the accuracy of the interacting method varies with temperature and interaction parameters.

Abstract: Purified Gibbs states provide a bridge between finite-temperature physics, dissipative dynamics, and ground-state methods. In this work, we study the exact finite sum-of-squares (SoS) construction of their parent Hamiltonians and the associated Lindbladian based on modular annihilators. Given a finite set of Hermitian generators, the corresponding modular annihilators yield a frustration-free SoS representation without continuous time integrals or an explicit decomposition into Bohr-frequency sectors. The purified Gibbs state remains a common zero mode while the freedom to choose and combine the generators can be used to optimize the spectral properties of the parent Hamiltonian. For free-fermion Hamiltonians, modular transformations act linearly on Majorana operators, leading to an analytically solvable family of parent Hamiltonians parameterized by a real symmetric coefficient matrix \(S\). For the scalar-functional subclass $S=f(h)$, we show that, at fixed operator norm, the choice $S_{\mathrm{opt}}\propto 1/\sqrt{\cosh(2βh)}$ has mixing time upper bound $2\log(2N/ε)$ for any $β$, which exhibits rapid mixing and is irrelevant to the inverse temperature $β$. For interacting systems, where the modularly dressed generators are not available in closed form, we introduce a Krylov--Lanczos approximation scheme and bound the resulting ground-state error in terms of the modular-approximation error and the parent-Hamiltonian gap. Numerical results illustrate the free-fermion spectral advantage and show how the accuracy of the interacting construction depends on temperature, interaction strength, and Krylov dimension.

</details>


### [105] [Efficient biphoton generation by a waveguide-coupled single atom](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30276)
*Mao-Hua Wang,Xiao-Jun Zhang,M. Artoni,G. C. La Rocca,Jin-Hui Wu*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A single atom near a chiral waveguide enhances biphoton generation via spontaneous four-wave mixing, enabling efficient photon channeling and meeting key metrics for quantum light sources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study aims to improve biphoton generation efficiency without relying on loss suppression or strict phase matching, addressing challenges in on-chip quantum light sources.

Method: A perturbative treatment for a four-level atomic system is developed, focusing on atomic decay rates affecting biphoton generation into guided versus unguided modes.

Result: Optimal generation rates are achieved, comparable to those of large atomic ensembles in free space, validating the effectiveness of the proposed approach.

Conclusion: The biphoton source is compact, scalable, and robust, satisfying essential requirements for future quantum technologies.

Abstract: A single atom undergoing spontaneous four-wave mixing near a chiral waveguide can efficiently channel an emitted Stokes-anti-Stokes photon pair into two tightly confined waveguide modes, yielding thus enhanced biphoton generation without requiring loss suppression or stringent phase matching. We develop a perturbative treatment, valid for a four-level atomic system under experimentally realistic conditions, to explain physical origins and clarify relevant constraints of such an enhancement determined by the interplay of atomic decay rates toward guided and unguided modes. Besides achieving optimal generation rates equivalent to a cold atomic ensemble hundreds of micrometers long in free space, our biphoton source naturally fulfills key requirements for next-generation on-chip quantum light sources, namely low-loss operation, robustness, compactness, and scalability.

</details>


### [106] [Best Annealing Path of Quantum Annealing via Efficient Adiabatic Phase Transition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30282)
*Kiyotaka Murashima*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents Nested Simulated Annealing (NSA), a method inspired by Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC), for enhancing Quantum Annealing (QA) efficiency via classical and quantum hybrid computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the challenges in Quantum Annealing related to small energy differences causing increased transition probabilities, necessitating lower operational temperatures.

Method: NSA, inspired by QMC, treats spins as continuous variables, deriving formulas for total energy with problem and perturbation Hamiltonians in the context of non-stoquastic Hamiltonians.

Result: Illustrates a clear link between local-maxima and convergence speed in computations, showing that optimal annealing paths lead to faster convergence with minimal resource use.

Conclusion: Demonstrates an effective methodology for parameter selection in simulating QMC on classical computers, potentially optimizing adiabatic phase transitions.

Abstract: Quantum Annealing (QA) has already put into practical use and considered useful for solving many social issues, such as reduction of traffic congestion and delivery optimization. But in QA, when the energy difference between the ground state and the first excited state is small, the transition probability between them increases. Therefore, in order to decrease the transition, QA has to be performed at extremely low temperatures. To simulate the situation, it has the problem in that it takes much time. Many studies are underway to accelerate QA theoretically, one of which is to incorporate non-stoquastic Hamiltonian. On the other hand, I proposed Nested Simulated Annealing (NSA) inspired by Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC). I showed the computational speedup could be achieved dramatically, by considering the idea that the effect of flipping a spin preferentially influenced the spins directly interacting with it. Although NSA was based on such classical concept of causality, the hybrid computation both quantum and classical approach worked well. In this paper, in order to discuss the relationship between NSA and non-stoquastic Hamiltonian like XX-interaction, the spins are treated as continuous variables. I derive the formula to calculate the total energy in both with a problem Hamiltonian and the perturbation Hamiltonian induced by a transverse electromagnetic field. And I will show a clear relationship between local-maxima and the convergence speed when calculating it with the binary spin. More precisely, the annealing path with the smallest local-maxima converges fast, even though it consumes fewer computational resources. Therefore, it is possible to induce an optimal adiabatic phase transition by selecting NSA parameters appropriately. This paper shows an effective method to choose the parameters when simulating QMC on a classical computer.

</details>


### [107] [Indirect stabilization of N -level quantum systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30308)
*Francesca Carlotta Chittaro*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper addresses the stabilizability of N-level quantum systems via indirect control using a noisy ancilla, with a focus on systematic conditions for two-level systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore the stabilizability of multi-level quantum systems, given the complex challenges posed by indirect control through noise.

Method: Develop a comprehensive theoretical framework for N-level systems, with a detailed analysis of the necessary and sufficient conditions for asymptotic stability specifically in two-level systems.

Result: Identification of complete conditions for asymptotic stability in two-level quantum systems, demonstrated through numerical simulations converging to a Bell state.

Conclusion: Indirect control via a noisy ancilla can effectively stabilize two-level quantum systems under defined conditions.

Abstract: We study the stabilizability of a N -level quantum systems using an indirect control ar- chitecture mediated by a noisy ancilla. After outlining the general theoretical framework for arbitrary N , we focus on the two-level case (N = 2), for which we derive a complete set of necessary and sufficient conditions for asymptotic stability. Finally, we illustrate the analyti- cal results with a simple numerical example on a two-spin system, showing convergence to a Bell state for a set of random initial conditions.

</details>


### [108] [Quantum-interference metrology of dissipative Kerr solitons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30313)
*Yun-Ru Fan,Yong Geng,Ji Liu,Yong-Jun Huang,Hai-Zhi Song,Hao Li,Li-Xing You,Heng Zhou,Kun Qiu,Kai Guo,Guang-Can Guo,Qiang Zhou*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a novel method for characterizing dissipative Kerr solitons in optical microresonators using quantum interference, bypassing common distortions from amplification and dispersion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Characterizing the intrinsic temporal structure of dissipative Kerr solitons is challenging due to low pulse energy and the need for complex amplification and dispersion management in conventional methods.

Method: The authors employ a quantum-interference metrology technique based on Hong-Ou-Mandel interference to measure soliton pulses at the single-photon level, obtaining pulse durations without amplification or dispersion compensation.

Result: The method accurately retrieves near transform-limited pulse durations, even after transmission through 25 km of standard fiber, and allows for sub-picosecond characterization of temporal separations in multi-soliton states through additional interference features.

Conclusion: This quantum-inspired metrology offers a robust, distortion-free approach to studying the temporal dynamics of solitons, providing a significant advancement in soliton characterization in optics.

Abstract: Dissipative Kerr solitons in optical microresonators underpin chip-scale frequency combs with applications ranging from coherent telecommunications to precision spectroscopy. Yet the characterization of their intrinsic femtosecond temporal structure remains challenging, as the low pulse energy and broad spectral bandwidth necessitate optical amplification and careful dispersion compensation in conventional ultrafast diagnostics, both of which can significantly distort the waveform. Here we demonstrate a quantum-interference metrology of microcomb solitons based on Hong-Ou-Mandel interference. By attenuating the soliton stream to the single-photon level and measuring fourth-order interference, we directly retrieve near transform-limited pulse durations without amplification or dispersion management, remaining accurate even after propagation through 25 km of standard fiber. The same interferogram also provides direct access to the temporal separations in multi-soliton states by converting inter-soliton separations into additional interference dips at corresponding delays, enabling sub-picosecond characterization of their intracavity temporal structure. This quantum-inspired paradigm introduces a fundamentally new metrological approach that is immune to amplification and dispersion distortions, offering a powerful tool for the characterization of complex soliton physics.

</details>


### [109] [Exact quantum splitting and the structure of finite algebras](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30340)
*Muhammad Imran*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents an unconditional quantum algorithm for factoring squarefree polynomials over finite fields, utilizing linear algebra and amplitude amplification to ensure deterministic results, and extends to separating commutative algebras into their simple components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide an efficient quantum algorithm for factoring polynomials and to achieve certainty without reliance on conjectures like the Extended Riemann Hypothesis, improving upon existing randomized methods.

Method: The algorithm implements an exact quantum approach using single-qubit rotations and a counting argument, allowing the use of nonconstant test elements derived from quadratic-character and absolute-trace tests, with amplitude amplification ensuring probability success of at least 1/2.

Result: The algorithm requires $r-1$ quantum splitting rounds and operates with $O(n^3	ext{log }q)$ quantum operations while successfully deterministically achieving the desired algebraic decompositions.

Conclusion: The algorithm not only factors squarefree polynomials but also provides the radical and minimal two-sided ideals decomposition for any $n$-dimensional associative $	ext{F}_q$-algebra, thus enhancing computational efficiency and certainty in algebraic structures.

Abstract: Berlekamp's algorithm factors a squarefree polynomial $f\in\mathbb{F}_q[x]$ by deterministic linear algebra, reducing the problem to splitting an explicit commutative algebra $B\cong\mathbb{F}_q^r$ into its $r$ simple factors. For large odd $q$, the standard efficient splitting step is randomized, while known derandomizations are conditional on the Extended Riemann Hypothesis. We give an unconditional exact quantum implementation in a circuit model permitting single-qubit rotations through efficiently computable angles. The construction uses an unconditional counting argument. For a block containing $s\ge2$ irreducible factors, a quadratic-character test in odd characteristic and an absolute-trace test in characteristic $2$ yield a nonconstant test element with probability $p_{q,s}\ge\tfrac12$, known exactly in advance and depending only on $q$ and $s$, not on the unknown factorization. Exact amplitude amplification therefore converts each randomized test into a procedure succeeding with certainty after one amplification iteration. The resulting algorithm uses exactly $r-1$ quantum splitting rounds and $O(n^3\log q)$ quantum $\mathbb{F}_q$-operations and $O(n^3)$ classical operations, requiring no primitive root, quadratic non-residue, or distinct-degree preprocessing. The method also splits arbitrary finite-dimensional separable commutative $\\mathbb{F}_q$-algebras given by structure constants. Combined with R'onyai's classical structure theory, which computes the radical deterministically and reduces the remaining tasks deterministically to polynomial factorization, it yields the radical and the Wedderburn decomposition of $A/\mathrm{Rad}(A)$ into minimal two-sided ideals, with certainty, for any $n$-dimensional associative $\mathbb{F}_q$-algebra given by structure constants, using $O(n^4\log q)$ quantum $\mathbb{F}_q$-operations.

</details>


### [110] [Quantum Imaginary Time Evolution on an Infinite 1D Chain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30363)
*Hao-Ti Hung,Tung Tsao,Ying-Jer Kao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a quantum-circuit algorithm for imaginary-time evolution of infinite one-dimensional systems, utilizing a parameterized circuit to optimize ground-state energy, validated through simulations and noise analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop an efficient quantum algorithm for imaginary-time evolution in quantum many-body systems, enhancing ground-state energy estimations.

Method: The ITE algorithm is derived from the time-dependent variational principle, incorporating a parameterized quantum circuit and the quantum Lanczos algorithm for improved energy estimates.

Result: Benchmarking on the transverse-field Ising model shows results from both classical simulations and quantum devices, with a statistical analysis of cost function and energy distributions highlighting noise effects.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates the algorithm's effectiveness and the significant impact of sampling noise on convergence in quantum measurements.

Abstract: We introduce a quantum-circuit algorithm for performing imaginary-time evolution on infinite one-dimensional lattice systems. The method uses a parameterized quantum circuit to represent a uniform matrix product state ansatz. We derive the ITE algorithm using the time-dependent variational principle and employ the quantum Lanczos algorithm to improve the ground-state energy estimate. As a benchmark, we simulate the transverse-field Ising model using both classical simulators and IBM Quantum devices. Our analysis includes a statistical study of the distributions of the cost function and energy density obtained from quantum measurements, illustrating the effects of finite-sampling noise on convergence.

</details>


### [111] [Marginal spectral distributions on regular bipartite unitary orbits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30370)
*Lin Zhang*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper investigates the probability distributions of spectra in bipartite density matrices by randomizing eigenbases, focusing on joint characteristic functions, derivations for qubit and qutrit cases, and employing advanced mathematical techniques.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how the spectra of reduced density matrices behave when the eigenbasis is randomized while fixing the spectrum of a bipartite density matrix.

Method: The approach uses the Harish-Chandra-Itzykson-Zuber (HCIZ) integral, along with confluent determinants and various mathematical tools to derive joint density functions for the marginal states in different subsystems (qubits and qutrits).

Result: The paper derives specific density functions for the Bloch radii, as well as the largest and smallest eigenvalues of marginal states, showcasing their piece-wise polynomial nature based on fixed global eigenvalues.

Conclusion: These findings align with the Duistermaat-Heckman theorem and enhance the understanding of spectral distributions in quantum systems.

Abstract: Fix the spectrum of a bipartite density matrix and randomize its eigenbasis according to Haar measure. We study the probability distributions induced on the spectra of the two marginal states. For arbitrary subsystem dimensions $m$ and $n$, the joint characteristic function of the reduced density matrices is expressed as a Harish-Chandra-Itzykson-Zuber integral whose external eigenvalues are the pairwise sums $x_i + y_j$. Repeated external eigenvalues are handled by confluent determinant limits. In the two-qubit case, we derive an explicit alternating-spline formula for the joint density of the two marginal Bloch radii. Its support is the Bravyi-Klyachko compatibility region. We also obtain a compact truncated-power formula for the Bloch-radius density of either individual qubit marginal. In the qubit-qutrit case, we derive a truncated-power formula for the qubit Bloch-radius density and a bivariate spline formula for the joint density of the largest and smallest eigenvalues of the qutrit marginal. The latter two variables determine the full qutrit spectrum because the trace is fixed. The derivations combine confluent HCIZ integrals, distributional Fourier inversion, orbital measures, and the SU(2) and SU(3) derivative principles. The resulting densities are piece-wise polynomial on chambers determined by subset sums of the fixed global eigenvalues, in agreement with the Duistermaat-Heckman description of projected coadjoint-orbit measures.

</details>


### [112] [Cross-Spectral Reservoir Correlations as a Resource for Finite-Time Quantum Otto Engines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30496)
*Siddhartha Dutta,Sujay Mondal,Ankush Das,Anumita Mukhopadhyay,Abhijit Bandyopadhyay*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This study explores how longitudinal-transverse cross-spectral correlations in reservoirs affect the performance of a finite-time quantum Otto engine, revealing their potential to enhance power output and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the impact of correlation effects in quantum thermal machines, particularly in non-Markovian dynamics.

Method: Utilizing a second-order time-convolutionless approach, we derive isochoric dynamics and analyze the system using a Bloch-vector representation to capture cycle dynamics.

Result: Cross-spectral correlations can boost output power and influence the transient behavior of the engine, while the limit-cycle efficiency remains aligned with the Otto value for certain strokes.

Conclusion: Off-diagonal reservoir spectra provide a novel resource for optimizing the performance of finite-time quantum thermal machines.

Abstract: We investigate the thermodynamic consequences of longitudinal-transverse cross-spectral reservoir correlations in a finite-time quantum Otto engine with a two-level working medium. Each reservoir couples through excitation-relaxation and dephasing channels whose fluctuations are characterized by a Hermitian positive-semidefinite spectral-density matrix, with the off-diagonal elements encoding their cross correlations. The finite-time isochoric dynamics is derived within the second-order time-convolutionless framework, without imposing the Markov limit at the outset, so that finite reservoir-memory effects can enter through time-dependent dissipative and reservoir-induced coherent contributions. The resulting dynamics is then recast in Bloch-vector form to construct the stroke-resolved cycle dynamics. At fixed auto-spectral densities, cross-spectral correlations modify the populations and coherences of the working medium and thereby its thermodynamic performance. Increasing the correlation strength can enhance the output power, with the enhancement controlled by the cross-spectral phase and characteristic frequency scale. The correlations also reshape the transient cycle-to-cycle evolution and the approach to periodic operation, while the limit-cycle efficiency remains fixed at the Otto value for the population-preserving unitary strokes considered here. These results establish off-diagonal reservoir spectra as an additional resource for controlling finite-time quantum thermal machines.

</details>


### [113] [Efficient measurement schemes for the Monte Carlo projective quantum eigensolver](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30612)
*Divye Baid,Maria-Andreea Filip*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The MC-PQE offers reduced measurement costs for energy estimation in quantum computing, and extends existing techniques for joint measurement to improve accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enhance measurement efficiency in quantum eigensolvers by leveraging techniques developed for VQE while addressing the unique requirements of MC-PQE.

Method: The study assessed various measurement techniques for joint estimation of asymmetric expectation values in MC-PQE, particularly focusing on Pauli term grouping and measurement allocation across molecular systems.

Result: Full commuting Pauli term grouping with tailored measurement allocation yielded a 5-10× reduction in standard error compared to traditional methods, outperforming classical shadows techniques for the tested systems.

Conclusion: The integration of advanced measurement strategies significantly enhances the performance of MC-PQE, making it more efficient for molecular systems in quantum computing.

Abstract: The Monte Carlo Projective Quantum Eigensolver (MC-PQE) was recently introduced as an alternative to hybrid algorithms like the variational quantum eigensolver (VQE). By using a quantum Monte Carlo-inspired scheme for energy estimation, MC-PQE was found to decrease the required measurement cost relative to comparable conventional approaches. However, in the context of VQE, numerous techniques have been developed to reduce the measurement overhead by joint measurement of multiple observable. In this work, we extend these approaches to the asymmetric expectation values required in MC-PQE and assess the performance of different techniques for various molecular systems of up to 12 qubits. We find full commuting Pauli term grouping combined with tailored measurement allocation techniques leads to a 5-10$\times$ reduction in standard error for the same total number of quantum measurements. Conventional Hamiltonian grouped measurement outperforms tractable classical shadows tomography based techniques for the considered systems.

</details>


### [114] [Gate-Efficient Implementation of the Query-Optimal Time-Dependent Hamiltonian Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30629)
*Boyang Chen,Minbo Gao,Zhengfeng Ji,Tongyang Li,Xinzhao Wang,Shuo Zhou*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper improves the circuit implementation of a query-optimal Hamiltonian simulation algorithm, reducing gate overhead while maintaining query complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The existing implementation of the query-optimal algorithm incurs excessive gate overhead despite its efficiency in query complexity.

Method: The authors present a new circuit implementation that uses an exact dyadic factorization for efficient one- and two-qubit gate usage, while retaining the optimal query complexity.

Result: The proposed implementation requires $O[q (a + 	ext{log}(1 + rac{T(α+ βT)}{ε}))]$ gates instead of the significantly larger overhead in previous implementations.

Conclusion: This method effectively balances optimal query complexity with reduced circuit resource requirements, enhancing practicality for Hamiltonian simulations.

Abstract: The query-optimal algorithm of [CGWZ26] for general time-dependent Hamiltonian simulation uses $$
  q = O\left( αT +
  \frac{\log(1/\varepsilon)}{\log\left(e + \log(1/\varepsilon)/(αT) \right)}
  \right) $$ queries to $\mathrm{HAM\mbox{-}T}$ within $\varepsilon$ error for a Lipschitz-continuous time-dependent Hamiltonian $H(t)$ on $[0,T]$ satisfying $\left\lVert H(t)\right\rVert\leqα$. However, its direct circuit implementation incurs a substantially larger gate overhead. In this note, we give an implementation of the same algorithm that retains its optimal query complexity and uses $$
  O\left[ q \left( a + \log\left(1 + \frac{T(α+ βT)}{\varepsilon}
  \right) \right) \right] $$ one- and two-qubit gates, where $a$ is the number of block-encoding ancilla qubits and $β$ is the Lipschitz constant of $H$. The main ingredient is an exact dyadic factorization of the ordered update product in the underlying one-query transducer.

</details>


### [115] [Deterministic Universal Logical Gates for Finite-Energy GKP Qubits in Trapped Neutral Atoms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30631)
*Alok Kumar,Aaron N. Raja,Diksha Thapliyal,Ishitwa Kumar Das,Ajay Wasan1*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces a universal logic gate set for GKP qubits in neutral atoms, achieving high fidelity without measurements, using ancilla states and Rydberg interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To create a universal set of logical gates for finite-energy GKP qubits in neutral atoms, enabling efficient quantum control without intermediate measurements.

Method: Utilizing internal electronic states as ancillae for state-dependent displacements and Rydberg-mediated dipole interactions for controlled-Z gates, all under magic-trapping conditions.

Result: Achieved gate fidelities of 0.986 and 0.985 for single-qubit phase and controlled-Z gates, respectively, with potential improvements at lower squeezing parameters.

Conclusion: The proposed approach facilitates high-fidelity, measurement-free logical control of motional GKP qubits in neutral-atom systems, advancing quantum computing capabilities.

Abstract: We present a universal logical gate set for finite-energy Gottesman-Kitaev-Preskill (GKP) qubits encoded in the harmonic motional states of trapped neutral atoms. Internal electronic states serve as ancilla for implementing state-dependent conditional displacements in phase space, enabling arbitrary single-qubit phase gates. A Transient Rydberg excitation-mediated atomic dipole-dipole interaction enables a controlled-Z gate without invoking the blockade mechanism. The gates operate under magic-trapping conditions, allowing continuous trapping throughout the gate sequence, thereby preserving the motional encoding without intermediate measurement or feedforward. We assess the experimental feasibility of the proposed protocols using neutral $^{88}\mathrm{Sr}$ atoms and obtain average gate fidelities of $0.986$ for single-qubit phase gates and $0.985$ for the controlled-Z gate at a finite squeezing parameter of $Δ=0.25$ (12 dB of squeezing), increasing to $0.997$ and $0.995$ respectively as $Δ$ reaches $0.1$. These results provide a route toward universal, measurement-free logical control of motional GKP qubits in neutral-atom architectures.

</details>


### [116] [Trade-off between Cooling-Step Count and Geometric Implementation Cost in Non-Markovian Algorithmic Cooling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30660)
*Yohei Azumai,Yoshihiko Hasegawa*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper explores the trade-off between cooling performance and resource costs in quantum cooling, focusing on how reservoir memory influences these aspects in single-qubit heat-bath algorithmic cooling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding the resource costs associated with quantum cooling is crucial for efficient quantum computation, especially regarding how reservoir memory impacts these costs.

Method: The study utilizes a pseudomode mapping to represent non-Markovian dynamics in a repeated collision circuit, assessing the geometric implementation cost via matrix-based simulations and actual implementation on an ibm_kawasaki processor.

Result: The analysis reveals that reducing reservoir memory leads to fewer cooling steps but typically results in higher geometric costs for the cooling protocols.

Conclusion: This study provides valuable insights into reservoir engineering for algorithmic cooling by highlighting the resource-based trade-offs involved.

Abstract: Quantum cooling is important for reliable quantum computation but involves a trade-off between cooling performance and implementation resources. Although reservoir memory can improve particular aspects of cooling performance, the associated resource cost, particularly for circuit implementation, remains insufficiently understood. Here, we investigate how reservoir memory affects the trade-off between cooling performance quantified by the cooling-step count and geometric implementation cost in single-qubit heat-bath algorithmic cooling. Using a pseudomode mapping, we represent the non-Markovian damped Jaynes--Cummings dynamics by a repeated collision circuit and evaluate its geometric implementation cost. Using matrix-based simulations and an implementation on the ibm_kawasaki Heron r2 processor, we identify a trade-off: suppressing reservoir memory reduces the cooling-step count but generally increases the geometric protocol cost. Our work provides a resource-based perspective on reservoir engineering for algorithmic cooling.

</details>


### [117] [Entanglement-enabled Criticality in One-dimensional Quantum Contact Process](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30681)
*Ya-Xin Xiang,Tianyi Yan,Weibin Li,Yu-Qiang Ma*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This study reveals that the one-dimensional quantum contact process undergoes a distinct continuous absorbing-state phase transition characterized by unique critical exponents and highlights the importance of entanglement in understanding its dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To clarify the nature of the phase transition in the one-dimensional quantum contact process, which remains a topic of debate.

Method: The authors employ Liouvillian spectral analysis, the tensor jump method, exact quantum jump Monte Carlo simulations, and truncated Wigner simulations to investigate the 1D QCP.

Result: The study finds a continuous absorbing-state phase transition in the 1D QCP with distinct critical exponents, and notes that the Liouvillian gap closes below the critical point, questioning the reliability of spectral gap analysis in identifying phase transitions.

Conclusion: Entanglement, though weak in the 1D QCP, is essential for accurately capturing the critical behavior, contrasting with the predictions of semiclassical methods that suggest a spurious first-order transition.

Abstract: The contact process is a paradigmatic example of nonequilibrium dynamics, with broad applications ranging from chemistry to sociology. Its quantum counterpart, the quantum contact process (QCP), extends the classical model to include coherent processes. Despite sustained interest, the nature of the transition in the one-dimensional (1D) QCP remains debatable. Here, combining Liouvillian spectral analysis, the tensor jump method, exact quantum jump Monte Carlo, and truncated Wigner simulations, we show that 1D QCP undergoes a continuous absorbing-state phase transition, with critical exponents distinct from the classical case. We further find Liouvillian gap closes well below the critical point, highlighting that spectral gap analysis alone cannot distinguish a phase transition from metastability in the QCP. Crucially, the 1D QCP is weakly entangled, yet even this weak entanglement is indispensable for capturing the correct critical behavior, whereas semiclassical methods artificially stabilize the active state and predict a spurious first-order transition. Our work establishes the quantum origin of the phase transition in the 1D QCP and underscores the essential role of entanglement in dissipative quantum many-body systems.

</details>


### [118] [Flatness-Preserving Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30697)
*Otto Veltheim,Esko Keski-Vakkuri*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper explores antiflatness in quantum states, defines Flatness-Preserving Operations (FPOs), and establishes conditions for these operations in relation to Orthogonality-Preserving Operations (OPOs).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation lies in developing a resource theory for antiflatness in quantum states, expanding the understanding and classification of quantum operations.

Method: The study analyzes OPOs as isometric embeddings involving unitaries/isometries and shows the relation of FPOs to constant maps and specific OPOs.

Result: It is proven that FPOs can only map to fixed flat states or follow specific conditions where the appended state is flat, alongside a characterization of flat convex combinations of flat states.

Conclusion: The findings clarify the structure of FPOs and OPOs, advancing the study of quantum state properties and their operational potentials.

Abstract: A quantum state is called flat if it is proportional to a projector. There has been recent interest in studying antiflatness, the property of diverging from flat states, and establishing a resource theory for it. Identifying the free operations (the Flatness-Preserving Operations (FPOs)) remained an open problem. For this purpose, we first discuss Orthogonality-Preserving Operations (OPOs), of which trivial examples are unitary operations in an isolated system. More generally, we give a simple proof that all OPOs are isometric embeddings consisting of combinations of unitaries/isometries and appending a fixed state. We then show that all FPOs are either constant maps to some fixed flat state or a special case of an OPO, where the appended fixed state must be a flat state. We also show that the only possible flat convex combinations of flat states are those of orthogonal flat states with weights given by their purities.

</details>


### [119] [PauLie: Fast Classification of Pauli Dynamical Lie Algebras](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30771)
*Oxana Shaya,Konstantin Golovkin,Mainak Roy,Vincent Russo*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: PauLie is an efficient framework for determining the isomorphism type of dynamical Lie algebras (DLAs) generated by Pauli strings, significantly reducing computational time compared to brute-force methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explicit computation of DLAs is computationally demanding, and many applications only require knowledge of the isomorphism type, motivating the need for a more efficient solution.

Method: PauLie utilizes anticommutation-graph reduction to classify DLAs generated by Pauli strings in polynomial time, specifically $O(n|	ext{G}|	ext{max}(n,|	ext{G}|))$.

Result: PauLie transforms DLA classification into a routine preprocessing task, with demonstrated applications in Lie-algebraic simulation, diagnosing barren plateaus, and engineering optimal Pauli string generator sets.

Conclusion: PauLie enhances the efficiency of DLA analysis, making it practical for various quantum applications.

Abstract: The dynamical Lie algebra (DLA) governs the controllability, expressibility, and simulation complexity of a quantum system. Explicitly computing it has been a major computational bottleneck: brute-force Lie closure scales exponentially in the number of qubits $n$. Many applications, however, consult only the isomorphism type of the DLA. We introduce PauLie, an open-source framework that decides this isomorphism type for DLAs generated by arbitrary Pauli strings, building on the anticommutation-graph reduction of Aguilar et al. PauLie runs in $O(n|\mathcal{G}|\max(n,|\mathcal{G}|))$ time, where $|\mathcal{G}|$ is the number of generators, turning DLA classification into a routine preprocessing step. We demonstrate its use as a structural oracle for routing Lie-algebraic simulation and Cartan decomposition, diagnosing barren plateaus in variational quantum algorithms, and engineering universal Pauli string generator sets with optimal generation rate.

</details>


### [120] [Quantum Block Turbo Codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30775)
*Khaled Jebari,Luiz Anet Neto,Ramesh Pyndiah,Jean-Louis de Bougrenet de la Tocnaye*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper introduces a theory for quantum block turbo codes, an adaptation of turbo product codes to quantum error correction, detailing their iterative decoding algorithm and simulating performance on depolarizing channels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper builds on the foundation laid by turbo codes in classical error correction and aims to extend these principles to quantum error correction.

Method: The authors develop a theoretical framework for quantum block turbo codes, including an iterative decoding algorithm, and conduct performance simulations.

Result: Simulations demonstrate the effectiveness of quantum block turbo codes on a depolarizing channel using various constituent codes.

Conclusion: The introduction of quantum block turbo codes provides a promising new avenue for quantum error correction, enhancing the reliability of quantum communications.

Abstract: In the early nineties, the introduction of turbo codes revolutionized classical error correction. The idea was mainly applied on two types of codes: convolutional turbo codes and turbo product codes. The first type of codes was adapted to quantum error correction which initiated the theory of quantum serial turbo codes. In this paper, we present a theory for quantum block turbo codes, the quantum analog of the second type of turbo codes. We describe their iterative decoding algorithm and simulate their performances on a depolarizing channel for different constituent codes.

</details>


### [121] [Neutral atom quantum computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30783)
*M. Saffman*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper reviews the advancements in neutral atom qubits for quantum computing, highlighting their historical development, recent progress, and future potential.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a comprehensive overview of the performance and scalability advancements in neutral atom qubit technology for quantum computing.

Method: A review of the physics behind neutral atom qubits, along with a survey of current capabilities and achievements in the field.

Result: Notable advancements include demonstrations of quantum algorithms and the implementation of logical qubits for fault-tolerant error correction.

Conclusion: The paper outlines the significant progress made and suggests a positive outlook for the future development of neutral atom qubit technology.

Abstract: Neutral atom qubits are one of the leading approaches for implementation of a large scale quantum computer. The original proposals for neutral atom qubits were formulated more than 25 years ago, with first demonstrations of a universal gate set following 10 years later. In the last few years the performance and scale of neutral atom qubit arrays has developed at a rapid pace leading to demonstrations of quantum algorithms, and logical qubits for fault tolerant error correction. This contribution reviews the physics of the neutral atom approach, surveys current capabilities, and provides an outlook for future progress.

</details>


### [122] [Photon-efficient quantum repeater chains via hyperentanglement-assisted purification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30786)
*Vinay Kumar,Krishan Joshi,Claudio Cicconetti*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes the DAHR protocol, integrating DOF-Assisted Entanglement Purification with BDCZ, enhancing quantum entanglement distribution efficiency by reducing resource requirements compared to BBPSSW purification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the inefficiencies of traditional entanglement distribution protocols that require many resource-intensive purification rounds.

Method: Integrating DAEPP into the BDCZ scheme, resulting in the DAHR protocol, and deriving a closed-form end-to-end fidelity recursion.

Result: Numerical analysis shows that DAHR outperforms BBPSSW purification in terms of resource efficiency and fidelity at specific operational points.

Conclusion: DAHR offers a more efficient alternative for entanglement distribution in quantum networks, requiring fewer rounds of purification to achieve similar or better fidelity.

Abstract: Linear quantum repeater chains based on Werner-state purification (the BBPSSW protocol) and entanglement swapping (the BDCZ scheme) are fundamental to entanglement distribution in quantum networks. However, they operate under a stringent operation reliability threshold and rely on resource-intensive recurrence purification rounds, each consuming two entangled pairs to probabilistically produce one. In the literature, hyperentanglement has been proposed to exploit multiple degrees of freedom (DOF), such as polarisation and spatial modes, to encode independent entangled states within a single photon pair. This has led to the definition of DAEPP (DOF-Assisted Entanglement Purification Protocol), which we propose to integrate with the BDCZ scheme, resulting in a chain protocol that we call DAHR (DOF-Assisted Hyperentanglement Repeater). The DAEPP step distils the fidelity of a DOF by consuming other(s). In this work, we propose and analyse a DAHR variant which integrates DAEPP at every segment of an end-to-end path combined with BDCZ. We derive a closed-form end-to-end fidelity recursion that embeds single-segment DAEPP into the BDCZ scheme and give a strict resource lower bound for any BDCZ baseline utilising BBPSSW purification to match DAHR's per-segment effective fidelity. At a representative asymmetric operating point informed by prior experiment, we show numerically that matching DAHR's single-photon-pair performance requires two to three rounds of BBPSSW purification. Additionally, below a critical operation reliability, no amount of BBPSSW rounds matches DAHR's one DAEPP round performance.

</details>


### [123] [Metrological quantum-to-classical crossover in the volume of a noisy quasiperiodic lattice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30834)
*Priya Ghosh,Debarupa Saha,Ujjwal Sen,Debraj Rakshit*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The study examines the metrological performance of quantum sensors at the localization-delocalization transition, highlighting a quantum-to-classical crossover influenced by noise and system size, which limits quantum advantage in sensing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To analyze the metrological performances of quantum many-body sensors operating near localization-delocalization transitions.

Method: The Aubry-André-Harper model is investigated, focusing on how thermal noise and lattice imperfections affect the quantum Fisher information and its scaling with system size.

Result: Quantum-enhanced scaling occurs for small systems near the transition, but is lost beyond a characteristic system size that depends on noise; increased temperature or lattice imperfections leads to classical-limited scaling.

Conclusion: Noise mechanisms fundamentally restrict the scalability of quantum-enhanced sensing, where exceeding a noise-dependent size threshold eradicates quantum advantages.

Abstract: Localization-delocalization transitions have recently been proposed for building a class of efficient quantum many-body critical sensors. This work scrutinizes metrological performances of such devices by focusing on the Aubry-André-Harper model that supports a localization-delocalization transition at finite strength of the onsite potential. We identify a metrological quantum-to-classical crossover driven by the interplay between noise and system size, whereby quantum-enhanced scaling of the quantum Fisher information persists only up to a finite, noise-dependent characteristic system-size. We first consider thermal noise and show that, at and near the localization-delocalization transition, the quantum Fisher information exhibits quantum-enhanced scaling for small systems but the system is stripped of this advantage beyond the characteristic crossover length. The crossover length decreases with increasing temperature. We then consider imperfections in the lattice hopping strengths and find a qualitatively similar crossover. There, the quantum-enhanced regime, identified with super-extensive scaling, gives way to an extensive scaling-a classical-limited weaker form-at sufficiently large system sizes. Thus, distinct noise mechanisms lead to a common limitation on the scalability of quantum-enhanced sensing: increasing the probe size beyond a noise-dependent limit can destroy the metrological quantum advantage.

</details>


### [124] [Size-Independent Robustness in Multipartite Bell Self-Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30851)
*Shen Cao,Xingjian Zhang,Fei Shi,Qi Zhao*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a robust method for self-testing multipartite entanglement in large quantum systems, establishing a definitive bound that is scalable and efficient for high-dimensional entanglement verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous methods for self-testing multipartite entanglement were limited by error bounds that worsen as system size increases, hampering practical applications.

Method: The authors derive a linear, device-independent self-testing bound for n-qubit GHZ states that depends solely on the observed violation error, utilizing an operator-inequality framework for efficient numerical validation up to n=100.

Result: The proposed method allows for the certification of GHZ entanglement under consistent noise levels in large-scale systems, maintaining efficiency and accuracy.

Conclusion: This research enables scalable, practical device-independent verification of multipartite entanglement, overcoming previous restrictions tied to system size.

Abstract: Practical robust self-testing of multipartite entanglement has so far been restricted to small-scale systems due to error bounds that degrade severely with system size. In this work, we establish multipartite self-testing with robustness independent of the size of the quantum network. We derive a fully analytic, device-independent self-testing bound for $n$-qubit Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) states. The bound scales linearly with the observed violation error and lies universally within a constant factor of two from a theoretical upper bound. Furthermore, the operator-inequality framework reduces the verification of the conjectured optimal bound to a highly efficient numerical check, which we perform up to $n=100$. Consequently, GHZ entanglement can be certified under a fixed noise level in arbitrarily large systems, enabling scalable device-independent verification.

</details>


### [125] [Well-conditioned iterative methods for large open quantum systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30860)
*Gaspard Beugnot,Paul Gregory,Rémi Robin,Antoine Tilloy*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper proposes efficient iterative methods for computing steady states and excited states in Markovian open quantum systems modeled by the Lindblad Master Equation, leveraging a specific part of the equation that allows efficient inversion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the inefficiency associated with standard methods for computing the steady state and excited states of quantum systems governed by the Lindblad Master Equation, particularly for large dimension systems.

Method: The paper introduces an auxiliary completely positive trace-preserving map related to the inverse of the no-jump evolution part of the Lindblad equation, enabling efficient computation via iterative methods like Arnoldi iterations.

Result: The proposed method scales like O(n^3) per iteration, significantly improving computational efficiency for finding the steady state and low excited states on both CPU and GPU over traditional methods.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that the new approach not only reduces computational costs but also achieves competitive performance on stiff systems, suggesting its potential for practical applications in quantum system analysis.

Abstract: Markovian open quantum systems are well modeled by the Lindblad Master Equation (ME) $\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} t} ρ_t = \mathcal{L} ρ_t$, where $\mathcal{L}$ is a linear (super-)operator and $ρ_t$ is the system state, a positive matrix. When designing or characterizing a quantum system, one is usually interested in the steady state $ρ_\infty$ (such that $\mathcal{L} ρ_\infty = 0$), the first few excited states, and trajectories $t\mapsto ρ_t$. In finite dimension, $ρ_t$ is an $n\times n$ matrix, $\mathcal{L}$ thus typically costs $n^4$ to store explicitly as a dense matrix, and $O(n^6)$ to diagonalize or invert exactly, making standard linear algebraic techniques expensive for large systems. However, $\mathcal{L}$ usually costs only $O(n^3)$ to apply. This makes iterative methods appealing, but they do not work without a good preconditioner. In this article, our main observation is that a part of the Lindblad equation, corresponding to the so-called no-jump evolution $\mathcal{S}$, can be inverted efficiently. Using this inverse map, we introduce an auxiliary completely positive trace-preserving (CPTP) map $Φ$ whose fixed point is directly related to $ρ_\infty$, all the other eigenvalues having smaller magnitude. The map $Φ$ is thus well suited to iterative methods, and $ρ_\infty$ can be found in a few Arnoldi iterations. Using the same inverse map $\mathcal{S}^{-1}$ as preconditioner, we compute the low-lying spectrum efficiently via shift-invert Arnoldi, and, as a proof of concept, build an implicit time integrator that is competitive on stiff systems in the low-precision regime. For the steady-state and low excited states problems, our methods scale like $O(n^3)$ per iteration and offer state-of-the-art performance on CPU and GPU.

</details>


### [126] [Constrained minimax approximation for quantum signal processing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30937)
*Yulong Dong,James B. Larsen,Lin Lin,Rahul Sarkar*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: The paper addresses polynomial transformations in quantum signal processing through constrained minimax approximation, proposing methods to ensure feasibility across the entire domain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To efficiently implement polynomial transformations in quantum circuits through constrained minimax approximation while ensuring feasibility across the entire domain.

Method: It explores two approaches: a Remez exchange method with active-set constraint enforcement and nonlinear Fourier retraction, converting nearly feasible polynomials into feasible QSP polynomials without increasing their degree.

Result: The Remez method shows efficiency but lacks stability in some cases, while the Fourier retraction significantly preserves approximation accuracy and maintains effectiveness in unstable scenarios.

Conclusion: The proposed workflow effectively links classical minimax approximation, semi-infinite optimization, and nonlinear Fourier analysis, and is made available in the qsppack software package.

Abstract: Quantum signal processing (QSP) provides a simple and efficient framework for implementing polynomial transformations using quantum circuits. Its classical design stage leads to a constrained minimax approximation problem: find a polynomial of prescribed parity that approximates a target function uniformly on a fitting set while remaining bounded in magnitude by one on the domain $[0,1]$, which can be viewed as a semi-infinite constraint. Discretization converts the problem into a linear program, but feasibility at a set of finitely many sampled points does not ensure feasibility on the whole domain, especially when an optimal approximant reaches the boundary of the feasible set. We investigate two approaches to address this difficulty. A Remez exchange method combined with active-set constraint enforcement is efficient on many tested instances, but its stability depends on the target and problem geometry. We then introduce nonlinear Fourier retraction, which uses QSP completion and phase synthesis to turn a nearly feasible polynomial into phase factors for a feasible QSP polynomial without increasing the degree. Across representative problems, retraction largely preserves approximation accuracy and remains effective on instances where the Remez heuristic is unstable. The resulting workflow connects classical minimax approximation and semi-infinite optimization with nonlinear Fourier analysis, and is implemented in the qsppack software package.

</details>


### [127] [Work Extraction Across a Thermodynamic Hierarchy in Quantum Many-Body Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.31001)
*Akihiro Hokkyo,Masahito Ueda*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper explores the thermodynamic hierarchy of quantum many-body states, showing that a state can exhibit thermal and athermal characteristics at different tiers. Enlarging operational capabilities may enhance work extraction, constrained by the mutual-information entropy difference across levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
